1) What those statements mean—and why they matter for your approach
A) “Without enough interleaving, the model breaks”
What it means. The neat finite‑blocklength law

R

assumes (effectively) that successive channel uses are nearly independent. If the channel is correlated (block fading, colored noise), then uses inside a coherence tile share the same state 
H
H and are not independent. This adds correlation into the information density and inflates the variance term (“dispersion”), so the normal approximation with 
V
V from i.i.d. AWGN underestimates the redundancy you need.

Implication. You must spread each codeword across many independent time–frequency tiles (“interleave”) so that, at the codeword scale, the channel looks i.i.d. Ergodic behavior comes back; outage goes away; the normal‑approx law becomes predictive again.

In your flow. Because you’ll “normalize to signal property and response,” just bake interleaving into the abstraction: treat the code as operating over independent uses. Then check you have enough interleaving depth to justify that abstraction (bounds below).

C) “Without shaping/scrambling you leave capacity on the table”
What it means. There are two kinds of shaping:

Frequency/time shaping (a.k.a. water‑filling / pre‑whitening): if noise is colored with 
N
(
f
)
N(f), push power into the “cheaper” frequencies. Flat power is suboptimal.

Amplitude/probability shaping for finite constellations (PAS, shell/lattice shaping): match the input distribution to Gaussian‑like; this yields up to 1.53 dB shaping gain versus uniform signaling at high order.

Scrambling/interleaving here means randomizing placement of coded symbols in time–frequency to make each codeword see many independent states and to average interference.

In your flow. Treat frequency/time shaping as part of the “channel normalization” you said you’ll handle. Keep the shaping gap in mind: it’s an SNR margin you can budget (~1–1.5 dB) if you don’t probabilistically shape. Scrambling remains essential to justify i.i.d. modeling.

5) Why this fits your philosophy
You’re not repeating—you’re projecting into several “relationship layers” and coding across them. That’s precisely how modern capacity‑approaching schemes work (turbo/LDPC/protographs + interleavers).

By controlling

⋆

  failure)—orthogonal to your relationship layers.

3) Your multi‑scale “relationship streams” done right
You’re not repeating the same symbol—you’re generating independent projections at different scales (neighbors, diagonals, “two apart”, etc.) and multiplexing them across independent DOF. That is exactly what capacity‑approaching codes + interleaving do.

Two standard toolkits you can cite and use as design yardsticks:

4) “Pilot”/side information layers (your positional/spectral pilots)
Treat pilot layers as dedicated side‑information channels that (i) help you learn the local “transfer rule” (channel state, permutation/ablation map) and (ii) provide anchors that let your decoder entangle the latent structure with data layers.

Known guidance: in fading/unknown‑rule scenarios, a pilot density on the order of one per coherence tile (time–frequency patch where the rule is essentially constant) is typical; the training overhead scales with the number of unknowns per tile (e.g., antennas, or parameters of your “rule”). Classic results show optimal training length equals the number of unknown transmit dimensions if you can allocate power between data and pilots; overhead grows when you must share power equally. This supports your idea of a small, structured pilot set per scale. 
U of U Electrical Engineering
+1

How this answers your “min lower bound vs max lower bound”:

Minimum LB (optimistic): the capacity LB in step 3.

Realistic LB: the finite‑blocklength bound in step 4 (use it).

Conservative LB (“max LB”): add correlation/shaping slack and pilot overhead per step 5; if you skip probabilistic shaping, also budget ≈1–1.5 dB extra SNR to reach the same 
m
m. (That “shaping gap” is the classic 1.53 dB asymptotic limit.) 
arXiv

6) Why your specific construction is well‑founded
Your “two‑apart / diagonal / multi‑scale” streams are structured parity projections (not repetition). With proper interleaving, they behave like capacity‑approaching codes: they spread redundancy over independent DOF and thus achieve the bounds above.

Using covering arrays to schedule which relations to encode ensures you don’t waste tests and that you probe all required t‑wise constraints with near‑minimal 
N
N.

Absolutely—if you start with randomized probes (instead of a hard-to-construct covering array), you can still get clean, auditable laws for how many independent bits you accumulate as you add more probes. Below I formalize “a probe,” give a plug‑in information law (how many bits you gain), account for dependence from overlapping cells, and show coverage‑style results (how quickly you touch pairs / r‑tuples). I’ll finish with small, concrete numbers for an 11×11 board.

2) The Randomized‑Probe Information Law (with overlap penalty)
Two effects reduce additivity:

Overlapping content: probes that touch many of the same cells are statistically dependent even if their channel noises are independent.

Linear dependence (if your probes are linear): rank deficiency in the probe matrix.

# --- Probabilistic Probes Calculator ---
#
# What this does:
# 1) Estimates total puzzle entropy H(X) in bits (given colors & optional histogram/empties).
# 2) Computes per‑probe capacity C_probe for a q‑ary symmetric probe with error p_error.
# 3) Estimates bits accumulated with m randomized probes, correcting for expected overlap:
#       m_eff = m / (1 + (m-1)*gamma),   gamma = t / N,  t = cells touched per probe.
#    => I_total(m) ≈ min{ H(X),  m_eff * C_probe }.
# 4) For any target bit budget k (0..H), computes minimal probes m needed:
#       - Optimistic (no-overlap):             m_cap  = ceil(k / C_probe)
#       - Pessimistic (overlap-corrected):     m_ovlp = ceil( k*(1-γ) / (C - k*γ) ), if denominator>0, else ∞
# 5) Lets you vary:
#       - puzzle size (1x1 up to 256x256)
#       - number of colors (or custom histogram)
#       - probe alphabet q and error p_error
#       - probe footprint as either:
#            (a) a t-subset (t cells picked uniformly)  OR
#            (b) a square patch s x s with a fraction empty_in_patch (ablated)
# 6) Produces:
#       - A summary table for the scenario
#       - A table mapping desired bits k -> minimal probes (optimistic vs overlap)
#       - Curve: Bits learned vs number of probes (both optimistic and overlap)
#       - Saves CSVs for you to download
#
# How to use:
#   - Edit the CONFIG block below and re-run the cell.
#   - You can also generate sweeps across colors and puzzle sizes (see SWEEP_CONFIG).
#
# Notes:
#   - This is a *statistical* calculator; overlap model is conservative but auditable.
#   - If you prefer a denser model (e.g., RLNC/dense mixing), set t large and consider
#     that the rank penalty is small when m << N over large fields.
#
# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

import math
from typing import List, Optional, Dict, Any, Tuple
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from ace_tools import display_dataframe_to_user

# -----------------------
# CONFIG: edit as needed
# -----------------------

CONFIG = {
    # Puzzle geometry
    "puzzle_side": 11,          # 1 .. 256 (square)
    # Colors (either set 'num_colors' for uniform or provide 'color_histogram' that sums to 1 over active colors)
    "num_colors": 8,            # 1 .. 10 (ignored if color_histogram is provided)
    "color_histogram": None,    # e.g., [0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.10, 0.10, 0.15, 0.10, 0.10]; set to None for uniform
    "empty_fraction": 0.0,      # fraction of 'empty' cells in the whole puzzle (adds an extra symbol)
    # Probe/channel model
    "probe_q": 8,               # output alphabet size for probe (e.g., 2, 8, 16, 256)
    "probe_error": 0.05,        # symbol error probability for the probe channel (q-ary symmetric)
    # Probe footprint (choose one mode)
    "probe_mode": "square",     # "subset" or "square"
    # If mode == "subset":
    "subset_t": 9,              # t cells touched per probe (ignored if mode == "square")
    # If mode == "square":
    "patch_side": 3,            # s x s patch per probe
    "empty_in_patch": 0.0,      # fraction of cells inside a patch that are 'empty' (ablated) and carry no info
    # Desired probes & k grid for the output tables/plots
    "probes_max": 400,          # x-axis for bits-vs-probes plot & cap on search
    "k_points": 120,            # number of k-samples between 0 and H for the k->m table (kept manageable)
}

# Optional sweep (generate multiple scenarios of colors and sizes)
SWEEP_CONFIG = {
    "run_sweep": False,
    "puzzle_sides": [11, 32, 64, 128, 256],
    "num_colors_list": list(range(1, 11)),  # 1..10
    "probe_q": 8,
    "probe_error": 0.05,
    "probe_mode": "square",
    "patch_side": 3,
    "empty_in_patch": 0.0,
}


# -----------------------
# Core calculators
# -----------------------

def entropy_per_cell(num_colors: int,
                     color_histogram: Optional[List[float]] = None,
                     empty_fraction: float = 0.0) -> float:
    """
    Per-cell entropy in bits. If color_histogram is given, it's the distribution over active colors (sums to 1).
    Empty fraction adds an 'empty' symbol with probability z=empty_fraction.
    Remaining mass (1-z) is distributed according to color_histogram or uniformly over num_colors.
    """
    eps = 1e-12
    z = max(0.0, min(1.0, float(empty_fraction)))
    if color_histogram is not None:
        p = np.array(color_histogram, dtype=float)
        p = np.clip(p, 0.0, 1.0)
        if p.sum() <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Provided color_histogram sums to zero.")
        p = p / p.sum()  # normalize
        c = len(p)
    else:
        c = int(num_colors)
        if c <= 0:
            return 0.0
        p = np.ones(c) / c

    # Build the full distribution including 'empty' (if any)
    if z > 0:
        probs = np.concatenate(([z], (1 - z) * p))
    else:
        probs = p.copy()

    # Entropy in bits
    probs = np.clip(probs, eps, 1.0)  # avoid log(0)
    H = -np.sum(probs * np.log2(probs))
    return float(H)


def puzzle_entropy_bits(puzzle_side: int,
                        num_colors: int,
                        color_histogram: Optional[List[float]],
                        empty_fraction: float) -> Tuple[int, float]:
    """
    Return (N, H_total_bits) for a square puzzle of side 'puzzle_side'.
    """
    N = int(puzzle_side) ** 2
    H_cell = entropy_per_cell(num_colors, color_histogram, empty_fraction)
    return N, N * H_cell


def q_ary_capacity(q: int, p_error: float) -> float:
    """
    Capacity (bits per probe) of a q-ary symmetric channel with symbol error p_error.
    C = log2(q) - [h2(p) + p*log2(q-1)]
    """
    q = int(q)
    if q < 2:
        return 0.0
    p = float(p_error)
    # For a q-ary symmetric channel, valid p is in [0, 1 - 1/q]. Beyond that, capacity is zero.
    p_max = 1.0 - 1.0 / q
    if p < 0:
        p = 0.0
    if p >= p_max:
        return 0.0

    def h2(x):
        x = max(1e-15, min(1 - 1e-15, x))
        return -x * math.log2(x) - (1 - x) * math.log2(1 - x)

    C = math.log2(q) - (h2(p) + p * math.log2(q - 1))
    return max(0.0, float(C))


def effective_t_and_gamma(N: int, cfg: Dict[str, Any]) -> Tuple[int, float]:
    """
    Compute t (cells touched per probe) and gamma = t / N, based on mode.
    - 'subset': t = subset_t
    - 'square': t = round((patch_side^2) * (1 - empty_in_patch))
    """
    mode = cfg.get("probe_mode", "square")
    if mode == "subset":
        t = int(cfg.get("subset_t", 1))
    else:
        side = int(cfg.get("patch_side", 1))
        empty_in_patch = float(cfg.get("empty_in_patch", 0.0))
        total_cells = side * side
        active_cells = int(round(total_cells * max(0.0, min(1.0, 1.0 - empty_in_patch))))
        t = max(1, active_cells)
    t = max(1, min(N, t))
    gamma = t / float(N) if N > 0 else 0.0
    return t, gamma


def m_eff(m: int, gamma: float) -> float:
    """
    Overlap-corrected effective independent probes:
    m_eff = m / (1 + (m - 1) * gamma)
    """
    if m <= 0:
        return 0.0
    return m / (1.0 + (m - 1.0) * gamma)


def bits_accumulated(m: int, H_total: float, C_probe: float, gamma: float) -> float:
    """
    I_total(m) ≈ min{ H_total,  m_eff * C_probe } with m_eff overlap-corrected.
    """
    return min(H_total, m_eff(m, gamma) * C_probe)


def invert_m_for_k_pessimistic(k_bits: float, C_probe: float, gamma: float) -> float:
    """
    Solve k ≤ C * m / (1 + (m - 1) * gamma) for m (pessimistic overlap model).
    Algebra gives:
        m = k*(1 - gamma) / (C - k*gamma)
    Require denominator > 0. If ≤ 0, return np.inf (cannot reach k under this model).
    """
    if k_bits <= 0:
        return 0.0
    if C_probe <= 0:
        return math.inf
    denom = (C_probe - k_bits * gamma)
    if denom <= 0:
        return math.inf
    m_val = (k_bits * (1.0 - gamma)) / denom
    # numerical safety
    if m_val < 0:
        return math.inf
    return m_val


def invert_m_for_k_optimistic(k_bits: float, C_probe: float) -> float:
    """
    Solve k ≤ m * C for m (no-overlap optimistic bound).
    """
    if k_bits <= 0:
        return 0.0
    if C_probe <= 0:
        return math.inf
    return k_bits / C_probe


def unique_cells_touched_expectation(N: int, t: int, m: int) -> float:
    """
    Expected number of *distinct* cells touched after m probes, each touching t cells uniformly without replacement.
    E[unique] = N * (1 - (1 - t/N)^m)
    """
    if N <= 0:
        return 0.0
    return N * (1.0 - (1.0 - t / float(N)) ** m)


# -----------------------
# Build the scenario
# -----------------------

def build_scenario_tables(cfg: Dict[str, Any]) -> Tuple[pd.DataFrame, pd.DataFrame]:
    side = int(cfg["puzzle_side"])
    N, H_total = puzzle_entropy_bits(
        puzzle_side=side,
        num_colors=cfg["num_colors"],
        color_histogram=cfg["color_histogram"],
        empty_fraction=cfg["empty_fraction"],
    )

    C_probe = q_ary_capacity(cfg["probe_q"], cfg["probe_error"])
    t, gamma = effective_t_and_gamma(N, cfg)
    probes_max = int(cfg["probes_max"])
    k_points = int(cfg["k_points"])

    # Summary
    H_cell = entropy_per_cell(cfg["num_colors"], cfg["color_histogram"], cfg["empty_fraction"])
    summary = pd.DataFrame([{
        "puzzle_side": side,
        "N_cells": N,
        "num_colors": cfg["num_colors"] if cfg["color_histogram"] is None else len(cfg["color_histogram"]),
        "H_cell_bits": H_cell,
        "H_total_bits": H_total,
        "probe_q": cfg["probe_q"],
        "probe_error": cfg["probe_error"],
        "C_probe_bits": C_probe,
        "probe_mode": cfg["probe_mode"],
        "t_cells_per_probe": t,
        "gamma_overlap = t/N": gamma,
    }])

    # k-grid and minimal probes
    # Use a manageable grid: 0..H_total in k_points steps (inclusive)
    if H_total <= 0:
        ks = np.array([0.0])
    else:
        ks = np.linspace(0.0, H_total, num=max(2, k_points))

    m_opt = [invert_m_for_k_optimistic(k, C_probe) for k in ks]
    m_pes = [invert_m_for_k_pessimistic(k, C_probe, gamma) for k in ks]

    # Ceil to integers, with guard for inf
    m_opt_int = [math.inf if (not np.isfinite(v)) else math.ceil(v) for v in m_opt]
    m_pes_int = [math.inf if (not np.isfinite(v)) else math.ceil(v) for v in m_pes]

    km_table = pd.DataFrame({
        "k_bits": ks,
        "m_needed_optimistic_no_overlap": m_opt_int,
        "m_needed_pessimistic_overlap": m_pes_int,
    })

    # Bits vs probes curve (for plotting)
    ms = np.arange(1, probes_max + 1, dtype=int)
    bits_opt = np.minimum(H_total, ms * C_probe)
    bits_pes = [bits_accumulated(int(m), H_total, C_probe, gamma) for m in ms]
    uniq_cells = [unique_cells_touched_expectation(N, t, int(m)) for m in ms]

    curve = pd.DataFrame({
        "m_probes": ms,
        "bits_optimistic_no_overlap": bits_opt,
        "bits_pessimistic_overlap": bits_pes,
        "expected_unique_cells_touched": uniq_cells,
    })

    return summary, km_table, curve


# -----------------------
# Run scenario & show
# -----------------------

summary_df, km_df, curve_df = build_scenario_tables(CONFIG)

# Display tables nicely in the UI
display_dataframe_to_user("Scenario summary", summary_df)
display_dataframe_to_user("Desired bits k → minimal probes m", km_df)
display_dataframe_to_user("Bits vs probes curve (and unique cells touched)", curve_df)

# Save CSVs for download
summary_path = "/mnt/data/probes_summary.csv"
km_path = "/mnt/data/probes_k_to_m.csv"
curve_path = "/mnt/data/probes_bits_vs_probes.csv"
summary_df.to_csv(summary_path, index=False)
km_df.to_csv(km_path, index=False)
curve_df.to_csv(curve_path, index=False)

# -----------------------

# Re-run with robust display: fall back to printing heads if display_dataframe_to_user fails.

import math
from typing import List, Optional, Dict, Any, Tuple
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

try:
    from ace_tools import display_dataframe_to_user
    HAVE_DF_VIEW = True
except Exception:
    HAVE_DF_VIEW = False

# Reuse previous definitions by checking if they exist; if not (fresh kernel), redefine.
def ensure_defined(name, fn):
    if name not in globals():
        globals()[name] = fn

# ---- from previous cell (redefine compactly if needed) ----
def entropy_per_cell(num_colors: int,
                     color_histogram: Optional[List[float]] = None,
                     empty_fraction: float = 0.0) -> float:
    eps = 1e-12
    z = max(0.0, min(1.0, float(empty_fraction)))
    if color_histogram is not None:
        p = np.array(color_histogram, dtype=float)
        p = np.clip(p, 0.0, 1.0)
        if p.sum() <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Provided color_histogram sums to zero.")
        p = p / p.sum()
        c = len(p)
    else:
        c = int(num_colors)
        if c <= 0:
            return 0.0
        p = np.ones(c) / c
    if z > 0:
        probs = np.concatenate(([z], (1 - z) * p))
    else:
        probs = p.copy()
    probs = np.clip(probs, eps, 1.0)
    H = -np.sum(probs * np.log2(probs))
    return float(H)

def puzzle_entropy_bits(puzzle_side: int,
                        num_colors: int,
                        color_histogram: Optional[List[float]],
                        empty_fraction: float) -> Tuple[int, float]:
    N = int(puzzle_side) ** 2
    H_cell = entropy_per_cell(num_colors, color_histogram, empty_fraction)
    return N, N * H_cell

def q_ary_capacity(q: int, p_error: float) -> float:
    q = int(q)
    if q < 2:
        return 0.0
    p = float(p_error)
    p_max = 1.0 - 1.0 / q
    if p < 0:
        p = 0.0
    if p >= p_max:
        return 0.0
    def h2(x):
        x = max(1e-15, min(1 - 1e-15, x))
        return -x * math.log2(x) - (1 - x) * math.log2(1 - x)
    C = math.log2(q) - (h2(p) + p * math.log2(q - 1))
    return max(0.0, float(C))

def effective_t_and_gamma(N: int, cfg: Dict[str, Any]) -> Tuple[int, float]:
    mode = cfg.get("probe_mode", "square")
    if mode == "subset":
        t = int(cfg.get("subset_t", 1))
    else:
        side = int(cfg.get("patch_side", 1))
        empty_in_patch = float(cfg.get("empty_in_patch", 0.0))
        total_cells = side * side
        active_cells = int(round(total_cells * max(0.0, min(1.0, 1.0 - empty_in_patch))))
        t = max(1, active_cells)
    t = max(1, min(N, t))
    gamma = t / float(N) if N > 0 else 0.0
    return t, gamma

def m_eff(m: int, gamma: float) -> float:
    if m <= 0:
        return 0.0
    return m / (1.0 + (m - 1.0) * gamma)

def bits_accumulated(m: int, H_total: float, C_probe: float, gamma: float) -> float:
    return min(H_total, m_eff(m, gamma) * C_probe)

def invert_m_for_k_pessimistic(k_bits: float, C_probe: float, gamma: float) -> float:
    if k_bits <= 0:
        return 0.0
    if C_probe <= 0:
        return math.inf
    denom = (C_probe - k_bits * gamma)
    if denom <= 0:
        return math.inf
    m_val = (k_bits * (1.0 - gamma)) / denom
    if m_val < 0:
        return math.inf
    return m_val

def invert_m_for_k_optimistic(k_bits: float, C_probe: float) -> float:
    if k_bits <= 0:
        return 0.0
    if C_probe <= 0:
        return math.inf
    return k_bits / C_probe

def unique_cells_touched_expectation(N: int, t: int, m: int) -> float:
    if N <= 0:
        return 0.0
    return N * (1.0 - (1.0 - t / float(N)) ** m)

# Use the same CONFIG as defined earlier in the first run, but recreate if missing
if "CONFIG" not in globals():
    CONFIG = {
        "puzzle_side": 11,
        "num_colors": 8,
        "color_histogram": None,
        "empty_fraction": 0.0,
        "probe_q": 8,
        "probe_error": 0.05,
        "probe_mode": "square",
        "subset_t": 9,
        "patch_side": 3,
        "empty_in_patch": 0.0,
        "probes_max": 400,
        "k_points": 120,
    }

def build_scenario_tables(cfg: Dict[str, Any]) -> Tuple[pd.DataFrame, pd.DataFrame, pd.DataFrame]:
    side = int(cfg["puzzle_side"])
    N, H_total = puzzle_entropy_bits(side, cfg["num_colors"], cfg["color_histogram"], cfg["empty_fraction"])
    C_probe = q_ary_capacity(cfg["probe_q"], cfg["probe_error"])
    t, gamma = effective_t_and_gamma(N, cfg)
    probes_max = int(cfg["probes_max"])
    k_points = int(cfg["k_points"])

    H_cell = entropy_per_cell(cfg["num_colors"], cfg["color_histogram"], cfg["empty_fraction"])
    summary = pd.DataFrame([{
        "puzzle_side": side,
        "N_cells": N,
        "num_colors": cfg["num_colors"] if cfg["color_histogram"] is None else len(cfg["color_histogram"]),
        "H_cell_bits": H_cell,
        "H_total_bits": H_total,
        "probe_q": cfg["probe_q"],
        "probe_error": cfg["probe_error"],
        "C_probe_bits": C_probe,
        "probe_mode": cfg["probe_mode"],
        "t_cells_per_probe": t,
        "gamma_overlap = t/N": t / float(N) if N > 0 else 0.0,
    }])

    # k grid
    if H_total <= 0:
        ks = np.array([0.0])
    else:
        ks = np.linspace(0.0, H_total, num=max(2, k_points))

    m_opt = [invert_m_for_k_optimistic(k, C_probe) for k in ks]
    m_pes = [invert_m_for_k_pessimistic(k, C_probe, t / float(N) if N > 0 else 0.0) for k in ks]
    m_opt_int = [math.inf if (not np.isfinite(v)) else math.ceil(v) for v in m_opt]
    m_pes_int = [math.inf if (not np.isfinite(v)) else math.ceil(v) for v in m_pes]

    km_table = pd.DataFrame({
        "k_bits": ks,
        "m_needed_optimistic_no_overlap": m_opt_int,
        "m_needed_pessimistic_overlap": m_pes_int,
    })

    ms = np.arange(1, probes_max + 1, dtype=int)
    bits_opt = np.minimum(H_total, ms * C_probe)
    gamma = t / float(N) if N > 0 else 0.0
    bits_pes = [bits_accumulated(int(m), H_total, C_probe, gamma) for m in ms]
    uniq_cells = [unique_cells_touched_expectation(N, t, int(m)) for m in ms]
    curve = pd.DataFrame({
        "m_probes": ms,
        "bits_optimistic_no_overlap": bits_opt,
        "bits_pessimistic_overlap": bits_pes,
        "expected_unique_cells_touched": uniq_cells,
    })
    return summary, km_table, curve

summary_df, km_df, curve_df = build_scenario_tables(CONFIG)

# Try nice display, otherwise print heads
if HAVE_DF_VIEW:
    try:
        display_dataframe_to_user("Scenario summary", summary_df)
        display_dataframe_to_user("Desired bits k → minimal probes m", km_df)
        display_dataframe_to_user("Bits vs probes curve (and unique cells touched)", curve_df)
    except Exception as e:
        print("Display failed; showing heads instead.\n", e)
        print("\nScenario summary:\n", summary_df)
        print("\nDesired bits k → minimal probes m (head):\n", km_df.head(10))
        print("\nBits vs probes curve (head):\n", curve_df.head(10))
else:
    print("\nScenario summary:\n", summary_df)
    print("\nDesired bits k → minimal probes m (head):\n", km_df.head(10))
    print("\nBits vs probes curve (head):\n", curve_df.head(10))

Estimates the entropy in bits needed for a given puzzle (size × colors × optional histogram/empties).

9) Practical reading of the formulas

Why it fits your construction.

Your “relationship layers” (neighbors, diagonals, two-apart) are exactly small-arity factors. If you make a systematic + parity design (QC-LDPC/protograph style), your decoder is vanilla BP.

Your interleaving/scrambling creates a large-girth/locally-tree-like graph, the textbook condition where BP is accurate (density evolution / EXIT analysis).

Randomized probes become random factors: by controlling overlap (via sequential covering arrays or your randomized schedule), you get expansion + low short cycles → BP-friendly.

How to argue with your matrices.

Whitening/sphering of feature messages ≈ making the check compatibilities isotropic → better conditioning of log-likelihood messages.

Fisher info / Gram: larger spectral gap of the factor graph’s non-backtracking operator (or of 
G
G in a linearized view) correlates with faster BP contraction.

Hessian/Jacobian: BP fixed points are stationary points of the Bethe free energy; local stability involves Jacobian spectral radius of the BP update. Whitening reduces that radius.

Transformer bridge (KVQ).

Put variables and checks as tokens; restrict attention to graph edges.

Queries/keys implement compatibility scoring (log-potentials); values carry log-messages; softmax is the entropic mirror map (see §4).

A stack of such layers = “BP-as-attention”—an easy story for stakeholders who know Transformers.

When BP is the strongest argument.

Sparse local constraints (your case), large girth, controlled overlap.

You want an LDPC-class argument (“near-capacity with density evolution”).

You want a clean conceptual bridge to Transformer inference as (learned) message passing.

5) How sequential covering arrays (SCA) help each method
Let your probe schedule define a (bi)partite graph or measurement matrix 
A
A.

For BP (LDPC): SCAs give large girth (few 4-cycles) and good expansion → density evolution predictions hold, messages are weakly correlated → faster, more reliable BP convergence.

For OAMP/AMP: SCAs with randomized phases/placements + whitening make the singular value spectrum well-behaved (right-orthogonal invariance), which is the core condition for state evolution to match reality.

For OMP: SCAs reduce expected overlaps → low mutual coherence

7) Which is the stronger argument for you?
Primary (most aligned with your story): Belief Propagation on an LDPC-like factor graph induced by your multi-scale parity/projection streams.

It is native to discrete colors, sparse locality, interleaving, and achieves near-capacity under classic assumptions you already enforce (low overlap, large girth).

It bridges cleanly to Transformers (BP-as-attention on the factor graph).

Your sphering/whitening + Fisher/Hessian language articulate why it converges fast and stably.

Co-primary when probes are dense or you want “assurance curves”: OAMP/AMP with a learned denoiser encoding your priors.

You can argue state evolution and show measured curves that match predictions—a powerful, quantitative story.

Whitening makes your measurement ensemble sit in the right-orthogonally invariant sweet spot.

Supportive baseline: OMP (especially for sparse violations or edit channels), with easy Gram/coherence guarantees.

Conceptual glue / pedagogy: Mirror Descent (variational view, attention’s softmax as entropic mirror map, natural gradient via Fisher).

If you want the clearest, standards-backed argument that dovetails with your parity/projection streams, interleaving, and Transformer narrative, lead with BP on an LDPC-style factor graph, and back it with OAMP for the dense/learned-denoiser layers. Great angle. If we reinterpret each symbol as a point on a rotating clock (phase on the unit circle) and allow ourselves to emit auxiliary sequences whenever the next symbol is “out of phase” (to carry priors, alignment and new information), the design questions become:

If the unknowns (e.g., phase drift) are compressible/sparse in some basis, how many compressed‑sensing‑style pilot measurements suffice?

C) “Out‑of‑phase → emit sequence” as differential/rateless layering
Your idea—when the next symbol is far “out of phase,” emit a small sequence that carries priors and “makes space” for the new phase—is well captured by two standard devices:

Differential coding (DPSK‑like): encode phase differences so absolute phase uncertainty becomes less harmful. This trades SNR for robustness; pilot needs drop, but symbol error rises for the same

F) Why this meshes with BP / OAMP
If your “out‑of‑phase → emit sequence” operates as local parity across neighboring carriers, your inference is a factor graph: BP decoding is the natural, near‑capacity choice; pilots anchor the factor potentials (phase).

If your carriers implement dense linear mixes (superpositions) of symbols (post‑whitening), OAMP/AMP with a small learned denoiser gives you state‑evolution predictions for convergence and MSE.

Now alternatively we can just fix our window width as the whole message and then we're just doing some density and size that might be easier to analyze. What I mean by that is like different ways of representing the same information. Now when we do that the message is likely going to be longer because we'll have to include relative positional data but a different way to think about that is instead of permuting the message we permute the samples and so we basically say hey this comes after this or this comes before this and again the density still comes into play so we have you know a thousand symbols that's not a realistic puzzle so let's say 20 by 20 which is 400 and let's say our density is whatever point something which corresponds to every time you pull 10 symbols and you include their relative positions and those symbols can be in any order which means you also need to indicate the direction and so how do we indicate that hey we've pulled enough to approach the information entropy that we need for both recall and for reasoning. Bottom line
“What is submodular here?”—Your information gained vs probe set is submodular if you (i) Gaussianize + whiten the probe features or (ii) use the concave‑over‑linear coverage→MI surrogate.

“To what extent can we enforce it?”—By design: randomized linear mixes (within windows), independent noise, whitening, and spacing/interleaving across windows.

“Did we pull enough?”—Use the submodular objective

Below is a compact blueprint that answers your core design question and gives you a proof‑friendly path:

Should we subsample at “any distance”, or at principled lengths (2, 4, 8, …), and how should we layer when the model can only absorb so many bits over so much span?

I’ll (1) compare the two strategies, (2) give the math you can plug in for guarantees (coverage, MI, union bounds, diminishing returns), (3) show how to layer elegantly under a bit‑budget, and (4) connect this to submodularity/log‑det, BP/AMP, expanders, “rotating clock” (phase), and your “pilot to restore dictionary/prefix” rule.

1) Two sampling strategies and the recommendation
A. Any‑distance random subsamples (one scale, full length)

Cons: proofs must track overlap correlations; you pay in redundancy if your true signal is mostly local.

Cons: you must decide how to allocate budget across scales—which we do below by a one‑line water‑filling rule.

Recommendation: use dyadic multi‑scale with random offsets as the backbone, and (optionally) add a thin “any‑distance” top‑layer (very low density) to catch any residual ultra‑long relations. This gives you the cleanest guarantees with the smallest proof surface.

6) Why long‑range sampling “goes to zero” after enough local sampling
Two complementary views:

Statistical mixing / decay. For many stationary sources (Markov chains above critical temperature, high‑temperature Ising, strongly‑mixing processes) the dependency decays: 
I
d
≤
C
 
e
−
d

d
​
  is negligible—no need to spend bits on very long distances.

Diagonalization / whitening. Your Gaussianization+whitening pushes the covariance toward diagonal; off‑diagonals beyond the coherence length become tiny. After whitening, long‑range linear structure is gone; any remaining higher‑order dependencies are weak and costlier than the near‑field ones. (Your “projecting into the diagonal” intuition is literally this Fisher/Hessian preconditioning.)

7) “Pilot tone” rule (restore dictionary/prefix when order breaks)
Your one‑liner belongs in every spec:

If a sketch breaks the local dictionary/ordering, emit a small pilot (positional or spectral) to re‑enter prefix space.

Proof‑wise, this does two things:
(i) it breaks symmetries (shift/reversal/conjugation) with 
O
(
log
⁡
N
)
O(logN) bits per block;
(ii) it reduces entropy of the latent alignment, which increases the per‑probe MI (log‑det goes up when alignment uncertainty goes down).

10) How fast does “submodular MI” saturate?
You have two independent “exponential” behaviors:

Union‑bound saturation (coverage): Gap at lag 
d
d decays as 
e
−
Λ
d
e 
−Λ

 . For log‑det with good whitening/expansion,

Both match the “near‑field / far‑field” intuition: fast gains early (local structure), slow tail (long range).

11) Connections (what to cite and how to talk about them)
BP / LDPC: your local windows define small‑arity factors (neighbors, “two‑apart”, etc.). With random offsets you get large girth / expansion, the regime where BP is near‑optimal; your “pilot tone” fixes factor alignment.

OAMP / AMP: your spectral sketches are (after whitening) linear; AMP with a small denoiser matches the state evolution story (predictable MI gains per iteration).

Expanders / zigzag: your random offsets make the probe‑cell bipartite graph an expander; coverage and mixing inherit expander bounds (another way to justify exponential saturation).

Compressive sensing: when the nuisance (phase/drift/rare edits) is sparse, pilot count follows 
m
≳
s
log
⁡
(
D
/
s
)
m≳slog(D/s).

Log‑det / determinant: MI in Gaussianized worlds is 
1
2
log
⁡
det
⁡
(
⋅
)
2
1
​
 logdet(⋅); your “diagonalize the permanent/determinant” intuition maps to preconditioning (whitening) that raises 
log
⁡
det
⁡
logdet.

Entropy (source vs phase): total entropy you must deliver = source entropy (symbol identities) + alignment/phase entropy (resolved by pilots).

Lempel–Ziv (resets): phrase boundaries in LZ‑parsing are a proxy for “dictionary resets”; high reset density ⇒ spend more on pilots / long‑range layers. (You don’t need a closed‑form; use it as an empirical meter to trigger pilots.)

13) Final answers to your two practical questions
“Any distance” vs “length‑2/4/8/… layers”?
Use dyadic layers with random offsets as the principled backbone; they’re easier to analyze, provably near‑optimal (submodular greedy), and they match the way dependence decays. If you’re worried about ultra‑long range, add a very low‑density global layer.

“Full width vs cascading roll‑ups vs ‘X within Y’”?
Do a pyramid: small‑
w
w layers first (dense, cheap), then roll up their histograms/sketches into larger 
w
w layers. That’s the simplest/elegant plan under a bit‑budget, and it’s exactly the setting where the union bound + submodularity + log‑det proofs are cleanest.

8) How this ties to BP / AMP / transformers / “near‑field” optics
BP on factor graphs. Each window gives local factors (short‑range); dyadic layers build a sparse, expanding factor graph with large girth. Submodular gains ↔ density evolution curves; your anchors/pilots fix global symmetries.

OAMP/AMP. If you emit dense linear mixtures (spectral/projected features) and whiten, state evolution predicts error vs layers/iterations; your exposure 
X

d
​
  is the “SNR budget” accumulated over scales.

KVQ/attention. Belief updates are entropic mirror‑descent steps (softmax); your prefix‑space anchors and dyadic “glimpses” make attention localized and then rolled up, matching how transformers build long‑range context from short‑range tokens.

“Isotropic transmitter + reflectors” analogy. Dyadic layers + anchors are designed reflectors/refractors that redirect energy (information) into the diagonal of the Gram/Fisher matrix (preconditioning). Whitening makes the Hessian close to identity: easy inference, near‑field decoding everywhere.

Below is a practical blueprint you can drop into a spec. I’ve split it into four parts:

Eyeball operators: what they are, how they saturate (subitizing/JND), and a minimal operator set.

Capability tests: how to measure a model’s breakpoints and how many pilot bits it needs.

1) “Eyeball” = subitizing + JND operators
Think of an eyeball operator as a primitive decision or statistic that the model can compute nearly at a glance, with accuracy that degrades past a small width. (Humans subitize exactly up to ~3–4 items; models have their own thresholds.) Each operator has a capacity curve: accuracy vs the amount of information in context.

Step 4 — Decoder equivalences

6) “Dictionary reset” proxies beyond LZ
LZ‑parsing density is a good, simple “reset/jump” proxy. Three stronger alternatives:

Context‑Tree Weighting (CTW) entropy rate: online and robust; spikes indicate resets.

Sequential change‑point tests on cross‑entropy (CUSUM or SPRT) using an adaptive Markov/PPM model; fires on distribution/phase shifts.

Excess entropy / block‑entropy slope: estimate 
H
(
L
)

Use these to trigger pilots/larger windows: when the reset meter spikes, emit a prefix/phase pilot and escalate 
w
w briefly.

7) Tying it back to your “optical” metaphor
Your reflector/refractor boosters are exactly matched‑filter + preconditioners that re‑focus energy onto the main diagonal of the Fisher/Hessian—another way to say whiten & sphere. Instead of cranking power on one beam (repetition), you add well‑placed reflectors (probes) that make independent, orthogonal looks; submodularity guarantees diminishing returns and the greedy policy tells you where to put the next reflector.

Below is a distilled blueprint that captures the core discussion, formulas, and algorithms (no code), written as an alternation between:

Principle — the clean statement you can prove and cite, and

Estimator/Design — the practical rule you use to size layers, pilots, and budget.

It is organized in the natural proof order for a KVQ transformer: make the measurements simple (Gaussian), make the geometry friendly (whiten/sphere), exploit submodularity (greedy, diminishing returns), certify reliability (union bound), and respect finite‑blocklength capacity. Throughout, the construction pushes phase entropy into real space (explicit pilot bits/estimates) so the imaginary part devolves to noise.

0) Objective in one sentence
We will recover and reason over a length‑
N
N message by emitting multi‑scale randomized sketches that (i) Gaussianize and whiten, (ii) are submodular in the information they add, and (iii) are layered to saturate the near‑field first, with a small top‑layer for tails. Pilots (positional/spectral) convert hidden phase entropy into explicit real bits, so that KVQ attention can operate in a well‑conditioned, nearly diagonal geometry.

8) KVQ transformer lens (how it’s implemented conceptually)
Principle 8 (attention as entropic mirror descent).
Softmax attention 
α
∝
exp
⁡
(
Q
K
⊤

⊤
 ) is the entropic mirror map (a multiplicative‑weights step). Whitening makes keys/queries isotropic (good Fisher/Hessian), pilots remove global symmetries so attention needn’t “hunt” for alignment. Local dyadic sketches → block‑sparse attention first, then roll‑ups extend range.

Variables/Acronyms: KVQ (Keys 
K
K, Values 
V

Estimator 8 (practical knobs).
Keep attention local first (small 
w
w), add anchors and spectral tags (pilots), then allow rolled‑up tokens to attend wider. This mirrors the greedy schedule of §5 (largest marginal MI per bit first).

13) One‑paragraph intuition (why this works)
You are turning implicit phase/ordering ambiguity (imaginary‑space entropy) into explicit, low‑bit real information (pilots), while gathering many independent, Gaussianized glimpses whose marginal information is diminishing (submodular). Dyadic layering concentrates spend where the source truly carries dependence (near field), and the union bound gives a crisp reliability certificate. In a KVQ transformer, this manifests as well‑scaled dot products (whitening), localized then rolled‑up attention (dyadic windows), and tiny anchors that keep the model in prefix space so that the residual uncertainty is easy to average away. The result is a short, auditable chain of proofs from Shannon (capacity), through log‑det submodularity, to Bethe/AMP geometry—exactly the sequence that lets you saturate your entropy and capacity budget with minimal fuss.

4) Edge cases that can break naïve claims (and how we box them)
4.1 Isomorphism / cospectral confusers
Gotcha. Cospectral but non‑isomorphic structures (same eigenvalues) defeat purely spectral signatures; Weisfeiler–Lehman (WL)‑type refinements fix many, but not all, cases.

5) Which operators map to determinant / permanent / cumulants (and why)
Think in two orthogonal families:

A) Reflectors (diagonalize / reduce off‑diagonals)
Covariance / Gram

⊤
 ).

Operator: whiten/sphere; project onto orthonormal bases (Fourier, Hadamard) inside windows.

Job: make the geometry Riemannian & Gaussian; power submodularity.

B) Refractors (expose higher‑order structure)
Third/fourth cumulants → bispectrum/trispectrum (Fourier domain cumulants).

Operator: compute randomized inner products of quadratic or cubic monomials of tapped symbols (kept small and Gaussianized via CLT).

Job: detect phase‑locked or parity‑locked patterns that covariance misses.

C) Permanent / Pfaffian surrogates (combinatorics you can actually compute)
Bethe permanent: the BP/Bethe free‑energy approximation to the permanent—natural for our factor‑graph view. It’s multiplicative, fast, and explains why BP often works on counting‑like tasks.

Kasteleyn/FKT (Pfaffian): for planar bipartite matchings, the number of perfect matchings is a Pfaffian (determinant of a skew‑symmetric matrix). When your latent constraints form such structure locally, you can compute it exactly—no enumeration.

Gurvits/van der Waerden lower bounds: give inequalities bounding the permanent by determinant‑like objects; they justify log‑det as a safe underestimator of combinatorial mass.

Why two operator families are necessary: reflectors (covariance/log‑det) “flatten” the metric; refractors (higher‑order cumulants) “reveal” latent alignment/parity that survives flattening. This duality is the formal reason we need at least two operator types.

Reasoned for 4m 26s
Below is an updated, distilled blueprint that folds in the editor‑pass fixes and makes all claims crisp, minimal, and auditable. I keep the Principle → Rule alternation (statement → how to enforce/measure) and define every symbol/acronym on first use. The goal is a short, Shannon‑style chain: Gaussianize → whiten/sphere → submodular gains (log‑det & coverage) → union‑bound certificate → finite‑blocklength capacity, with FPT fallback and Bethe/Pfaffian/Hafnian surrogates where exact combinatorics would be intractable. Throughout, “eyeball operators” are the primitive tests the model can do reliably inside a small window, and pilots push phase entropy (imaginary) into explicit real bits.

Principle 2.2 — Wick’s theorems and 2m‑th moments.
Bosonic/real Gaussian (classical): the 
2

Fermionic/Majorana Gaussian: the

Complex circular Gaussian: certain 2m‑th moments reduce to determinants.

What we can claim. After whitening (covariance 
V
≈
I
V≈I), these hafnian/pfaffian/determinant expressions factor into products of diagonal entries. Thus, our “collapse to the diagonal” shows up exactly: higher‑order moments reduce to products of variances, and off‑diagonal contributions vanish at the rate set by

δ 
off
​
 .

Kitaev‑chain lens (free fermions): correlators of Majorana modes are pfaffians of principal minors of the correlation matrix. A local Bogoliubov‑like (orthogonal) transformation block‑diagonalizes that matrix; our whitening plays the same role, driving pfaffians of minors toward the product of local variances (up to topological signs, which we can detect with tiny “parity” pilots).

Principle 2.3 — Permanent vs Bethe/pfaffian shortcuts.
The permanent is #P‑hard; exact counting is infeasible globally. But:

Bethe permanent (from BP/Bethe free energy) is a multiplicative surrogate tightly tied to our factor‑graph decoding.

Pfaffian (FKT/Kasteleyn) yields exact counts for planar perfect matchings; Hafnian appears in bosonic Gaussian optics (we never compute it globally, only as a feature inside small windows).

What we can claim. We never enumerate globally. In eyeball‑sized windows we (i) use pfaffian exact when planar, (ii) use Bethe permanent otherwise, and (iii) fall back to FPT enumeration limited by the operator width 
W
W. This preserves tractability while respecting the combinatorics.

One‑line takeaway
By Gaussianizing and whitening the sketches, we force all higher‑order structure to cumulants that vanish, reduce 2m‑th moments to hafnian/pfaffian/determinant forms that factor on the diagonal, and make both CFI and, in the Gaussian limit, QFI nearly diagonal (SLDs nearly commute). Submodular log‑det then certifies diminishing returns, dyadic exposure plus a union bound certifies coverage, and pilots push phase entropy into small, explicit real bits—bringing us into the regime where CSS hashing‑bound rates are approachable and any remaining combinatorics can be handled FPT‑locally.

A) Clarifications & precise positions
A1) “Beat the hashing bound by degeneracy”
The hashing bound (a random‑coding/coherent‑information lower bound) gives an achievable rate for Pauli‑type quantum channels using non‑degenerate random stabilizer codes.
Example (qubit depolarizing channel, error prob. 
p
p):

R

2
​
 (p/3)].
A degenerate code maps many distinct error patterns to the same syndrome. This can improve rate because the decoder does not need to distinguish between errors that are logically equivalent—i.e., the code “wastes” fewer bits on irrelevant distinctions.

Claim (standard): for several Pauli channels (including depolarizing in certain ranges), degenerate stabilizer/CSS codes achieve rates strictly above the non‑degenerate hashing bound. This does not beat the capacity; it beats the hashing lower bound because that bound is not tight when degeneracy is exploited.

Takeaway for us. Our pilotization + interleaving is designed to push the effective noise toward Pauli‑like, and our sparse parity architecture is degenerate by construction; so we can truthfully say we target hashing‑bound‑beating regimes (relative to the non‑degenerate bound), while staying within capacity.

A mixed‑radix basis is a combinatorial/arithmetic concept: e.g., Chinese Remainder / Smith Normal Form (SNF) decomposes integer modules into co‑prime invariant factors (radices) whose product equals

What we can claim precisely: equal eigenvalues do not imply a mixed‑radix basis. However, in our integer (quantized) design, SNF/CRT does realize a mixed‑radix decomposition; and our whitening pushes the real metric toward the same “all lanes equal” geometry that maximizes

A3) “Synergy”
In optimization, “synergy” is not standard; we’ll use two formal notions:

Partial Information Decomposition (PID) synergy: information present only in the joint of two features about a target, not in either alone (useful diagnostically).

In our pipeline, whitening + Gaussianization drives us toward submodularity (diminishing returns); persistent synergy flags higher‑order or matching‑type structure (i.e., time to switch to cumulant‑3/4 or matching/permanent tests locally).

C) Hashing bound, degeneracy, eigenvalues: the synthesis
Degeneracy ⇒ above hashing lower bound: Because degenerate codes can identify equivalence classes of errors rather than each error individually, their effective syndrome alphabet is smaller than the naive one; this shifts the random‑coding tradeoff and can yield rates above the non‑degenerate hashing bound (still below capacity).

Equal eigenvalues & majorization: A flat spectrum maximizes

⁡

Mixed‑radix (CRT/SNF) vs equal eigenvalues: SNF gives arithmetic “radices” (co‑prime invariant factors), while equal eigenvalues are geometric isotropy. We deploy both: CRT lanes for robust modular structure; whitening for isotropy—together they stabilize both symbol space and phase space.

E) Where new “matrix‑like” tools help (beyond moments)
Immanants (det/perm special cases): probe representation‑theoretic symmetry; diagnostics for when “perm‑like” weights dominate.

Matrix‑tree theorem (det of Laplacian minors): a determinant counts spanning trees—handy if a local constraint graph is tree‑like (then det, not perm, is the right count).

Mixed discriminants / Aleksandrov–Fenchel: quantify how a mixture of probes contributes to volume/log‑det; helpful to justify our greedy mixing and interleaving (spectral interlacing).

Fredholm determinant / hafnian for kernelized features: if you promote windows to integral kernels, these give the “continuous” analogues of our discrete counts.

Torontonian: when thresholded (on/off) bosonic‑style sketches are used, probabilities are torontonians rather than hafnians—useful surrogate.

F) Short answers to your pointed questions
“Beat the hashing bound by degeneracy?”
Yes, in the standard sense: degenerate stabilizer/CSS codes can achieve rates above the non‑degenerate hashing lower bound for several Pauli channels. We do this by design (degeneracy + pilotization), still below true capacity.

“Equal eigenvalues ⇒ mixed radix?”
No—as mathematics: equal eigenvalues mean isotropy (good for log‑det). “Mixed radix” is SNF/CRT (integer module decomposition). They are analogous but distinct. We use both: whitening (isotropy) + CRT lanes (co‑prime radices).

“When do I need cumulant vs permanent?”
Use cumulant‑2/log‑det when submodularity holds and higher cumulants are small (Gaussian path). Switch to permanent/hafnian/pfaffian (locally) when synergy persists or cumulants‑3/4 are large; then prefer pfaffian exact (planar), else Bethe permanent or FPT local enumeration.

One line you can quote
We run a two‑mode proof: reflector mode cancels off‑diagonals (Gaussianize + whiten ⇒ log‑det submodularity and diagonal moments); refractor mode handles the residual “matching mass” (permanent/hafnian/pfaffian locally, with Bethe/planar shortcuts and FPT windows). Degeneracy and pilots push us into hashing‑bound‑beating regimes while the union‑bound and log‑det gains certify coverage and efficiency.

Lemma 7 (Local matchings: perm/haf/pf).

Permanent counts weighted perfect matchings in bipartite graphs; #P‑hard globally.

Hafnian counts perfect matchings in general graphs (bosonic Gaussian moments).

Pfaffian computes perfect matchings exactly for planar graphs (FKT/Kasteleyn).
Hence, if the local “synergy graph” is planar and small, exact pfaffian is tractable; otherwise, use Bethe permanent (BP surrogate) or FPT enumeration restricted to windows 
≤

Proof sketch. Lemmas 1–5 supply the reflector path (Gaussianization ⇒ log‑det submodularity, dyadic coverage, union bound, MI bound; greedy optimality). Lemmas 6–8 trigger refractor handling and bound it to eyeball windows. Propositions 1–3 ensure pilots align phase/ordering (turning phase entropy into explicit bits), and that both the degeneracy advantage and finite‑blocklength budget are respected. Putting them together yields items (1)–(7).

7) Notes on “mixed‑radix” vs equal eigenvalues (one sentence)
Arithmetic diagonal: Smith Normal Form / Chinese Remainder Theorem decomposes integer constraints into co‑prime radices (mixed‑radix lanes).

Geometric diagonal: whitening drives real‑valued eigenvalues toward equality (isotropy), maximizing 
log
⁡
det
⁡
logdet at fixed trace.
They are distinct but complementary; we use both.

Final one‑liner
Alternate: use cumulant‑2/log‑det (reflector) whenever whitening makes the problem Gaussian and submodular; switch locally to matching/permanent (refractor) only when synergy survives. Interleave source and phase entropy by adding the smallest pilots that restore prefix/phase alignment. Allocate dyadic exposure to meet a union‑bound reliability target and stay within finite‑blocklength capacity. Any residual combinatorics is handled FPT‑locally. This yields provable recall and reasoning at the model’s capacity ceiling.

Below is a unified, editor‑tight recap of our conversation—now organized as a single, cohesive path from assumptions → formulas → decision rules → exemplars. 0) Aim & setting
Discussion ▸
We want maximum recall & reasoning under a fixed model capacity by alternating two lenses:

A cumulant‑2/log‑det (second‑order) lens that thrives after Gaussianization + whitening, giving submodular gains (clean greedy proofs).

A matching lens (permanent/hafnian/pfaffian) for joint‑only structure that survives second‑order flattening—handled locally in “eyeball‑sized” windows and approximated by Bethe or pfaffian when exact counting is hard.

We interleave source entropy (symbols) and phase/alignment entropy (pilots), working entirely with explicit tokens (CRT lanes from co‑prime/Singer sets for low coherence).

3) When second‑order is not enough: matching (“refractor”) path
Discussion ▸
If joint structure remains (e.g., “and + dog meaningful, each alone not”), second order can miss it. We detect this via a submodularity defect or third/fourth cumulants, then switch locally to matching objects: permanent (bipartite), hafnian (general), pfaffian (planar).

6) Factor graph & Bethe: how it’s computed “inside”
Discussion ▸
Our dyadic local rules define a factor graph with large girth (random offsets/CRT lanes). BP (sum‑product) approximately minimizes the Bethe free energy, which yields the Bethe permanent as a surrogate for counting. This is why local matching via Bethe fits naturally.

9) Scheduling & incoherence: CRT lanes + Singer sets
Discussion ▸
We schedule taps on CRT lanes (co‑prime moduli) and choose offsets via Singer difference sets to spread pairwise differences uniformly. This lowers mutual coherence and helps whitening and submodularity.

12) Final synthesis (one paragraph)
We Gaussianize and whiten dyadic sketches so second‑order (cumulant‑2) structure dominates; the information set function becomes log‑det, which is submodular, enabling greedy selection with 
(

CRT lanes and Singer‑set offsets maintain low coherence, reinforcing whitening and log‑det gains. The pipeline thus maximizes recall and reasoning at the model’s capacity ceiling with auditable certificates (union bound, Fano, finite‑blocklength) and a principled alternation between cumulant and permanent lenses.
